Analog Filter Prototypes & Order Formulas

To design an IIR filter, we first specify the passband ripple \( A_p \) (dB), stopband attenuation \( A_s \) (dB), and critical frequencies. We then calculate the order \( N \) of an analog filter prototype:

1. Butterworth maximally flat prototype

The magnitude squared response is:

\[ |H_a(j\Omega)|^2 = \frac{1}{1 + (\Omega / \Omega_c)^{2N}} \]

To meet bandedge specifications, the required filter order \( N \) is computed as:

\[ N = \frac{\log\left( \frac{10^{0.1 A_s} - 1}{10^{0.1 A_p} - 1} \right)}{2 \log(\Omega_s / \Omega_p)} \]

2. Chebyshev Type I (Ripple in passband)

\[ |H_a(j\Omega)|^2 = \frac{1}{1 + \epsilon^2 T_N^2(\Omega / \Omega_p)} \]

Where \( T_N(x) \) is the \( N \)-th order Chebyshev polynomial: \( T_N(x) = \cos(N \arccos x) \) for \( |x| \le 1 \). The order required is:

\[ N = \frac{\cosh^{-1}\left( \sqrt{\frac{10^{0.1 A_s} - 1}{10^{0.1 A_p} - 1}} \right)}{\cosh^{-1}(\Omega_s / \Omega_p)} \]
Page 174
Reference: Salivahanan Ch-8 (8.1, 8.3, 8.4) Poornachandra Ch-8 (8.1, 8.2, 8.3)

Lecture 11.2: IIR Design by Impulse Invariance

The core objective of the Impulse Invariance Method is to design a digital filter whose impulse response is a sampled copy of the analog filter's continuous impulse response:

\[ h[n] = T_s \cdot h_a(n T_s) \]

Let's derive the frequency-domain mapping relation. The Laplace transform of \( h_a(t) \) and Z-transform of \( h[n] \) are:

\[ H_a(s) = \int_{0}^{\infty} h_a(t) e^{-st} dt \quad \Rightarrow \quad H(z) = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} h[n] z^{-n} \]

Using Poisson's relation for sampling, the spectra are related by:

\[ H(e^{j\omega}) = \sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty} H_a\left(j \frac{\omega - 2\pi k}{T_s}\right) \]

This proves that the digital frequency response is a periodic summation of the analog frequency response. The mapping relates the variables via the transformation:

\[ z = e^{s T_s} \]

This maps the left-half s-plane (\( \text{Re}(s) \lt 0 \)) inside the unit circle (\( |z| \lt 1 \)), preserving stability.

Page 175
Reference: Salivahanan Ch-8 (8.1, 8.3, 8.4) Poornachandra Ch-8 (8.1, 8.2, 8.3)

Impulse Invariance Numerical Design Example

Problem: Design a digital filter using Impulse Invariance with sampling period \( T_s = 0.1 \text{ s}\), starting from the analog filter: \[ H_a(s) = \frac{2}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} \] Step 1: Perform Partial Fraction Expansion
Expand the transfer function into simple poles: \[ H_a(s) = \frac{A}{s + 1} + \frac{B}{s + 2} \] Using residues:
- For pole \( s_1 = -1 \): \( A = \lim_{s \to -1} \frac{2}{s+2} = 2 \)
- For pole \( s_2 = -2 \): \( B = \lim_{s \to -2} \frac{2}{s+1} = -2 \)
Thus: \[ H_a(s) = \frac{2}{s + 1} - \frac{2}{s + 2} \] Step 2: Map s-poles to z-poles
Each analog term of form \( \frac{A_i}{s - s_i} \) maps directly to the digital term \( \frac{T_s A_i}{1 - e^{s_i T_s} z^{-1}} \). Here:
- Pole \( s_1 = -1 \) maps to \( z_1 = e^{-1 \cdot 0.1} = e^{-0.1} = 0.9048 \)
- Pole \( s_2 = -2 \) maps to \( z_2 = e^{-2 \cdot 0.1} = e^{-0.2} = 0.8187 \)

Step 3: Assemble Digital Transfer Function \(H(z)\)
\[ H(z) = \frac{0.1 \cdot 2}{1 - 0.9048 z^{-1}} - \frac{0.1 \cdot 2}{1 - 0.8187 z^{-1}} \] \[ H(z) = \frac{0.2}{1 - 0.9048 z^{-1}} - \frac{0.2}{1 - 0.8187 z^{-1}} \] Simplifying: \[ H(z) = \frac{0.2(1 - 0.8187 z^{-1}) - 0.2(1 - 0.9048 z^{-1})}{(1 - 0.9048 z^{-1})(1 - 0.8187 z^{-1})} = \frac{0.0172 z^{-1}}{1 - 1.7235 z^{-1} + 0.7408 z^{-2}} \]
Page 176
Reference: Salivahanan Ch-8 (8.1, 8.3, 8.4) Poornachandra Ch-8 (8.1, 8.2, 8.3)

Impulse Invariance Limitation: Aliasing Proof

The relation \( z = e^{s T_s} \) maps multiple strips of the s-plane onto the unit circle of the z-plane. Specifically, any analog frequency point \( \Omega_k = \Omega_0 + \frac{2\pi k}{T_s} \) maps to the exact same digital frequency point:

\[ z = e^{j\Omega_k T_s} = e^{j(\Omega_0 T_s + 2\pi k)} = e^{j\Omega_0 T_s} \]

This many-to-one mapping folds the continuous frequency spectrum, creating aliasing distortion. If \( H_a(j\Omega) \) does not roll off to zero rapidly beyond the Nyquist band limit (\( |\Omega| \gt \pi/T_s \)), high-frequency noise will alias back into the passband.

⚠️ Important Design Note:
Because highpass and bandstop analog filters have high gain at high frequencies (as \( \Omega \to \infty \)), applying the Impulse Invariance method to these types results in severe, catastrophic aliasing. Impulse Invariance is strictly restricted to narrow lowpass and bandpass filter designs.
Page 177
Reference: Salivahanan Ch-8 (8.1, 8.3, 8.4) Poornachandra Ch-8 (8.1, 8.2, 8.3)

Lecture 11.3: Design by Bilinear Z-Transformation (BZT)

The Bilinear Z-Transformation (BZT) completely eliminates aliasing by mapping the entire analog frequency range \( -\infty \lt \Omega \lt \infty \) uniquely onto the unit circle range \( -\pi \lt \omega \lt \pi \).

Mathematical Derivation from Numerical Integration:
Let's model a continuous integrator: \( \frac{dy(t)}{dt} = x(t) \).
Integrating this differential equation from \( (n-1)T_s \) to \( n T_s \): \[ y(n T_s) - y((n-1)T_s) = \int_{(n-1)T_s}^{n T_s} x(t) dt \] Approximating the area under the curve using the trapezoidal rule: \[ y[n] - y[n-1] \approx \frac{T_s}{2} \Big( x[n] + x[n-1] \Big) \] Taking the Z-transform on both sides: \[ Y(z) \Big( 1 - z^{-1} \Big) = \frac{T_s}{2} X(z) \Big( 1 + z^{-1} \Big) \] The transfer function of the digital integrator is: \[ \frac{Y(z)}{X(z)} = \frac{T_s}{2} \cdot \frac{1 + z^{-1}}{1 - z^{-1}} \] In the Laplace domain, integration is \( 1/s \). Equating the Laplace and digital integrations: \[ \frac{1}{s} = \frac{T_s}{2} \cdot \frac{1 + z^{-1}}{1 - z^{-1}} \quad \Rightarrow \quad s = \frac{2}{T_s} \cdot \frac{1 - z^{-1}}{1 + z^{-1}} \] This is the Bilinear Z-Transformation equation.
Page 178
Reference: Salivahanan Ch-8 (8.1, 8.3, 8.4) Poornachandra Ch-8 (8.1, 8.2, 8.3)

BZT Frequency Warping Proof

To analyze the relationship between analog frequency \( \Omega \) and digital frequency \( \omega \), we substitute the points along the imaginary axis (\( s = j\Omega \)) and unit circle (\( z = e^{j\omega} \)):

Proof:
\[ j\Omega = \frac{2}{T_s} \cdot \frac{1 - e^{-j\omega}}{1 + e^{-j\omega}} \] Factor out half-angles in the numerator and denominator: \[ j\Omega = \frac{2}{T_s} \cdot \frac{e^{-j\omega/2} (e^{j\omega/2} - e^{-j\omega/2})}{e^{-j\omega/2} (e^{j\omega/2} + e^{-j\omega/2})} \] Using Euler's identity formulas: \[ j\Omega = \frac{2}{T_s} \cdot \frac{2j \sin(\omega/2)}{2 \cos(\omega/2)} \] Simplifying: \[ j\Omega = j \frac{2}{T_s} \tan\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right) \] Equating the imaginary components yields: \[ \Omega = \frac{2}{T_s} \tan\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right) \] This non-linear tangent compression maps the infinite analog axis to the finite digital circle.
Page 179
Reference: Salivahanan Ch-8 (8.1, 8.3, 8.4) Poornachandra Ch-8 (8.1, 8.2, 8.3)

Frequency Pre-Warping Necessity

The non-linear tangent compression curve wraps frequencies, distorting the relative spacing of bands. At low frequencies, mapping is nearly linear. However, at high frequencies, equal increments of digital frequency map to increasingly larger bands of analog frequency:

Fig: Non-linear Warping Curve \(\Omega = \frac{2}{T_s}\tan(\frac{\omega}{2})\)

To design an IIR filter that matches passband and stopband edges, we must apply **Frequency Pre-Warping** before designing the analog prototype:

\[ \Omega_p = \frac{2}{T_s} \tan\left(\frac{\omega_p}{2}\right) \quad \text{and} \quad \Omega_s = \frac{2}{T_s} \tan\left(\frac{\omega_s}{2}\right) \]

By mapping analog specifications using these pre-warped boundaries, the warping introduced by the final BZT step will restore critical frequencies back to their exact digital positions.

Page 180
Reference: Salivahanan Ch-8 (8.1, 8.3, 8.4) Poornachandra Ch-8 (8.1, 8.2, 8.3)

Detailed BZT Numerical Design Example

Problem: Design a stable 1st-order Butterworth digital lowpass filter with a 3dB cutoff frequency at \( \omega_c = 0.2\pi \). Assume sampling interval \( T_s = 1 \text{ second} \).

Step 1: Pre-warp the cutoff frequency
Using pre-warping formula: \[ \Omega_c = \frac{2}{T_s} \tan\left(\frac{\omega_c}{2}\right) = \frac{2}{1} \tan\left(\frac{0.2\pi}{2}\right) = 2 \tan(0.1\pi) = 2(0.3249) = 0.6498 \text{ rad/s} \] Step 2: Design 1st-order Analog Filter
The transfer function of a 1st-order analog lowpass filter is: \[ H_a(s) = \frac{\Omega_c}{s + \Omega_c} = \frac{0.6498}{s + 0.6498} \] Step 3: Apply Bilinear Z-Transformation
Substitute \( s = 2 \cdot \frac{1 - z^{-1}}{1 + z^{-1}} \) into \( H_a(s) \): \[ H(z) = \frac{0.6498}{2 \left( \frac{1 - z^{-1}}{1 + z^{-1}} \right) + 0.6498} \] Multiply numerator and denominator by \( 1 + z^{-1} \): \[ H(z) = \frac{0.6498 (1 + z^{-1})}{2(1 - z^{-1}) + 0.6498(1 + z^{-1})} \] \[ H(z) = \frac{0.6498 + 0.6498 z^{-1}}{(2 + 0.6498) + (-2 + 0.6498) z^{-1}} \] \[ H(z) = \frac{0.6498 + 0.6498 z^{-1}}{2.6498 - 1.3502 z^{-1}} \] Divide by 2.6498 to normalize the constant term to 1: \[ H(z) = \frac{0.2452 + 0.2452 z^{-1}}{1 - 0.5095 z^{-1}} \]
Page 181
Reference: Salivahanan Ch-8 (8.1, 8.3, 8.4) Poornachandra Ch-8 (8.1, 8.2, 8.3)

Lecture 11.4: Least-Squares and Optimal IIR Design

Prototype-mapping methods (Impulse Invariance and BZT) are restricted to standard filter forms (lowpass, highpass, etc.). For arbitrary frequency responses, we use optimization algorithms:

1. Prony's Method

An analytical least-squares time-domain optimization that models the IIR impulse response to match a desired sequence \( h_d[n] \). Let the IIR filter difference equation be:

\[ h[n] - \sum_{m=1}^{N} a_m h[n-m] = \sum_{k=0}^{M} b_k \delta[n-k] \]

By defining the equation error for \( n \gt M \):

\[ e[n] = h_d[n] - \sum_{m=1}^{N} a_m h_d[n-m] \]

Prony's method solves for the feedback coefficients \( a_m \) by minimizing the sum of squared errors \( \sum e^2[n] \) through a set of linear equations, and then solves for the feed-forward coefficients \( b_k \) directly.

2. Shanks' Method

An extension of Prony's method that refines the numerator coefficients. After solving for the feedback poles \( a_m \), the filter is represented as a cascade. The numerator coefficients \( b_k \) are re-optimized to minimize the squared error of the difference:

\[ E_{LS} = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} |h_d[n] - h[n]|^2 \]
Page 182
Reference: Salivahanan Ch-8 (8.1, 8.3, 8.4) Poornachandra Ch-8 (8.1, 8.2, 8.3)

Comparison Summary: IIR vs FIR Filters

Characteristic FIR Filters IIR Filters
Impulse Response Duration Finite (Causal, no feedback) Infinite (Recursive, feedback poles)
Stability Always stable Must be verified (poles inside unit circle)
Phase Can achieve exact linear phase Non-linear phase (group delay distortion)
Computational Complexity Higher order needed (more taps) Lower order needed (highly efficient)
Design Methods Windowing, Frequency Sampling, Remez Impulse Invariance, BZT, Least-Squares
[2024-Q1.c] [4 Marks] Easy Method Included Define BZT. Apply to \(H(s) = \frac{2}{(s+1)(s+2)}\) with \(T=1\text{ s}\) to find \(H(z)\).
Reference: Salivahanan Ch-8 (8.1, 8.3, 8.4) Poornachandra Ch-8 (8.1, 8.2, 8.3)

1. Bilinear Z-Transform (BZT):

The BZT is an algebraic mapping method used in digital filter design to map the analog transfer function \(H(s)\) in the continuous \(s\)-plane to the digital transfer function \(H(z)\) in the discrete \(z\)-plane. It is defined by the substitution:

\[s = \frac{2}{T} \frac{z-1}{z+1}\]

where \(T\) is the sampling interval. The BZT provides a one-to-one mapping that maps the entire imaginary axis of the \(s\)-plane onto the unit circle of the \(z\)-plane, mapping the stable left-half \(s\)-plane completely inside the unit circle of the \(z\)-plane, thereby preserving stability and avoiding frequency aliasing.

Figure 1.2: Mapping of continuous s-plane to discrete z-plane under BZT

2. Numerical Application:

Given the analog filter:

\[H(s) = \frac{2}{(s+1)(s+2)} = \frac{2}{s^2 + 3s + 2}\]

Applying BZT with sampling period \(T = 1\text{ s}\), we substitute:

\[s = \frac{2}{1} \frac{z-1}{z+1} = 2 \frac{z-1}{z+1}\]

Substituting this in \(H(s)\):

\[H(z) = \frac{2}{\left(2 \frac{z-1}{z+1}\right)^2 + 3\left(2 \frac{z-1}{z+1}\right) + 2}\] \[H(z) = \frac{2}{\frac{4(z-1)^2}{(z+1)^2} + \frac{6(z-1)}{z+1} + 2}\]

Multiplying numerator and denominator by \((z+1)^2\):

\[H(z) = \frac{2(z+1)^2}{4(z-1)^2 + 6(z-1)(z+1) + 2(z+1)^2}\]

Now, expand each term:

  • Numerator: \(2(z^2 + 2z + 1) = 2z^2 + 4z + 2\)
  • Denominator terms:
    • \(4(z^2 - 2z + 1) = 4z^2 - 8z + 4\)
    • \(6(z^2 - 1) = 6z^2 - 6\)
    • \(2(z^2 + 2z + 1) = 2z^2 + 4z + 2\)
  • Sum of Denominator terms: \((4+6+2)z^2 + (-8+4)z + (4-6+2) = 12z^2 - 4z\)

Thus, we obtain:

\[H(z) = \frac{2z^2 + 4z + 2}{12z^2 - 4z} = \frac{z^2 + 2z + 1}{6z^2 - 2z}\]

Expressing in terms of negative powers of \(z\) by dividing numerator and denominator by \(z^2\):

\[H(z) = \frac{1 + 2z^{-1} + z^{-2}}{6 - 2z^{-1}} = \frac{\frac{1}{6} + \frac{1}{3}z^{-1} + \frac{1}{6}z^{-2}}{1 - \frac{1}{3}z^{-1}}\]

Answer: \(H(z) = \frac{0.1667 + 0.3333z^{-1} + 0.1667z^{-2}}{1 - 0.3333z^{-1}}\)

Alternative Easy Exam Solution: 3-Step BZT Algebraic Shortcut

Setup:
Given \(H(s) = \frac{2}{(s+1)(s+2)}\) and sampling period \(T = 1\text{ s}\).
Standard Bilinear transformation mapping: \[s = \frac{2}{T}\frac{1-z^{-1}}{1+z^{-1}} = 2\frac{z-1}{z+1}\]

Step 1: Expand the analog denominator in polynomial form: \[D(s) = (s+1)(s+2) = s^2 + 3s + 2\]

Step 2: Substitute \(s = 2\frac{z-1}{z+1}\) and multiply numerator & denominator by \((z+1)^2\):
Numerator: \[N(z) = 2(z+1)^2 = 2(z^2 + 2z + 1) = 2z^2 + 4z + 2\] Denominator: \[D(z) = 4(z-1)^2 + 3(2)(z-1)(z+1) + 2(z+1)^2\] \[= 4(z^2 - 2z + 1) + 6(z^2 - 1) + 2(z^2 + 2z + 1)\] Collect like powers of \(z\): \[z^2: 4 + 6 + 2 = 12\] \[z^1: -8 + 0 + 4 = -4\] \[z^0: 4 - 6 + 2 = 0\] \[\implies D(z) = 12z^2 - 4z\]

Step 3: Assemble \(H(z)\) and express in standard negative powers \(z^{-1}\): \[H(z) = \frac{2z^2 + 4z + 2}{12z^2 - 4z} = \frac{1 + 2z^{-1} + z^{-2}}{6 - 2z^{-1}} = \frac{\frac{1}{6} + \frac{1}{3}z^{-1} + \frac{1}{6}z^{-2}}{1 - \frac{1}{3}z^{-1}}\]

DC Gain Verification Check (Exam Shortcut):

  • Analog DC Gain (\(s=0\)): \(H(0) = \frac{2}{(0+1)(0+2)} = \frac{2}{2} = 1.0\)
  • Digital DC Gain (\(z=1\)): \(H(1) = \frac{1 + 2(1) + 1}{6 - 2(1)} = \frac{4}{4} = 1.0 \quad \checkmark\)
  • Since \(H_a(0) = H_d(1) = 1\), the algebraic substitution is verified 100% correct!
[2024-Q1.d] [4 Marks] Easy Method Included Design analog Butterworth filter: -2dB passband attenuation at 20 rad/s, \(\le\) -10dB stopband at 30 rad/s.
Reference: Salivahanan Ch-8 (8.1, 8.3, 8.4) Poornachandra Ch-8 (8.1, 8.2, 8.3)

Step-by-step Design:

1. Extract Specifications:

  • Passband attenuation \(\alpha_p = 2\text{ dB}\) at passband frequency \(\Omega_p = 20\text{ rad/s}\).
  • Stopband attenuation \(\alpha_s = 10\text{ dB}\) at stopband frequency \(\Omega_s = 30\text{ rad/s}\).

2. Calculate Butterworth Filter Order \(N\):

The equation for order \(N\) is:

\[N \ge \frac{\log_{10}\left(\frac{10^{0.1\alpha_s} - 1}{10^{0.1\alpha_p} - 1}\right)}{2 \log_{10}\left(\frac{\Omega_s}{\Omega_p}\right)}\]

Calculate numerator terms:

\[10^{0.1\alpha_s} - 1 = 10^1 - 1 = 9\] \[10^{0.1\alpha_p} - 1 = 10^{0.2} - 1 = 1.5849 - 1 = 0.5849\]

Substitute these into the order formula:

\[N \ge \frac{\log_{10}\left(\frac{9}{0.5849}\right)}{2 \log_{10}\left(\frac{30}{20}\right)} = \frac{\log_{10}(15.387)}{2 \log_{10}(1.5)} = \frac{1.187}{2 \times 0.176} = \frac{1.187}{0.352} \approx 3.37\]

Rounding up to the nearest integer, we select \(N = 4\).

3. Calculate 3-dB Cutoff Frequency \(\Omega_c\):

\[\Omega_c = \frac{\Omega_p}{\left(10^{0.1\alpha_p} - 1\right)^{1/(2N)}} = \frac{20}{\left(0.5849\right)^{1/8}} = \frac{20}{0.9348} \approx 21.39\text{ rad/s}\]

4. Construct Analog Transfer Function \(H(s)\):

The poles of a normalized Butterworth filter for \(N=4\) are located on the unit circle at angles:

\[\theta_k = 180^\circ \pm \frac{(2k+1)180^\circ}{2N} \text{ for } k=0, 1\]

This gives normalized poles at angles \(112.5^\circ\), \(157.5^\circ\), \(202.5^\circ\), and \(247.5^\circ\). The normalized denominator polynomial is:

\[D(p) = (p^2 + 0.76537p + 1)(p^2 + 1.84776p + 1)\]

We de-normalize by replacing \(p\) with \(\frac{s}{\Omega_c} = \frac{s}{21.39}\):

\[H(s) = \frac{1}{\left[\left(\frac{s}{21.39}\right)^2 + 0.76537\left(\frac{s}{21.39}\right) + 1\right]\left[\left(\frac{s}{21.39}\right)^2 + 1.84776\left(\frac{s}{21.39}\right) + 1\right]}\] \[H(s) = \frac{\Omega_c^4}{(s^2 + 0.76537 \Omega_c s + \Omega_c^2)(s^2 + 1.84776 \Omega_c s + \Omega_c^2)}\]

Substitute \(\Omega_c = 21.39\) and \(\Omega_c^2 = 457.5\):

\[H(s) = \frac{209215}{(s^2 + 16.37s + 457.5)(s^2 + 39.52s + 457.5)}\]

Answer: \(H(s) = \frac{209215}{(s^2 + 16.37s + 457.5)(s^2 + 39.52s + 457.5)}\)

Alternative Easy Exam Solution: 4-Step Butterworth Design Shortcut

Design Checklist:

StepFormulaCalculationResult
1. Order \(N\) \(N \ge \frac{\log_{10}[(10^{0.1\alpha_s}-1)/(10^{0.1\alpha_p}-1)]}{2\log_{10}(\Omega_s/\Omega_p)}\) \(\frac{\log_{10}(9 / 0.5849)}{2\log_{10}(1.5)} = \frac{1.1872}{0.3522}\) \(N = \lceil 3.37 \rceil = 4\)
2. Cutoff \(\Omega_c\) \(\Omega_c = \frac{\Omega_p}{(10^{0.1\alpha_p}-1)^{1/(2N)}}\) \(\frac{20}{(0.5849)^{1/8}} = \frac{20}{0.9351}\) \(\Omega_c = 21.39\text{ rad/s}\)
3. Pole Angles \(\theta_k = 90^\circ + \frac{(2k+1)180^\circ}{2N}\) for \(k=0,1,2,3\) \(112.5^\circ, 157.5^\circ, 202.5^\circ, 247.5^\circ\) All poles in Left-Half \(s\)-plane
4. De-normalize Replace normalized \(p\) with \(s/\Omega_c\) Multiply 2nd-order biquads by \(\Omega_c^2 = 457.5\) \(H(s)\) fully constructed

Specification Consistency Check (Exam Shortcut):

  • Passband Attenuation at \(\Omega_p = 20\text{ rad/s}\):
    \(|H(j20)|^2 = \frac{1}{1 + (20/21.39)^8} = \frac{1}{1 + 0.5849} = 0.6309 \implies -10\log_{10}(0.6309) = 2.0\text{ dB} \quad \checkmark\)
  • Stopband Attenuation at \(\Omega_s = 30\text{ rad/s}\):
    \(|H(j30)|^2 = \frac{1}{1 + (30/21.39)^8} = \frac{1}{1 + 15.387} = 0.0610 \implies -10\log_{10}(0.0610) = 12.15\text{ dB} \ge 10\text{ dB} \quad \checkmark\)

Lecture 1 & 2 — DSP Foundations, Advantages & Representations
Core DSP Definition, Applications, 4 Representation Standards & Elementary Sequences
Source: DSP Class Lecture Notes • Pages 1–4 (19/07/26 – 23/07/26)

Exact Definitions & Taxonomy (Pages 1–2, 2024-Q5.a):

  • Definition of DSP: "Numerical manipulation of signal with the intention to measure, filter, produce, or compress continuous, analog sig."
  • Core Applications: Audio processing, Biomedical signal processing (ECG, EEG), Video compression, Nuclear explosion detection.
  • 5 Advantages of DSP over ASP: 1. Storing (indefinite storage without loss), 2. Lasting capacity (no component aging/drift), 3. Accuracy control (determined strictly by bit width), 4. Cheaper than analog, 5. Easily programmable.
  • 3 Key Disadvantages: (i) Reconstruction is digitally difficult, (ii) Time consuming (processing delays), (iii) Frequency limitation (constrained by ADC/DAC sampling speeds).
Real-Time DSP Block Diagram (Page 2):
\[\text{Analog Signal } x(t) \longrightarrow \boxed{\text{A/D Conv}} \xrightarrow{\text{digital signal } x[n]} \boxed{\text{Digital Processor}} \xrightarrow{\text{digital signal } y[n]} \boxed{\text{D/A Conv}} \longrightarrow \text{Analog Signal } y(t)\]

4 Discrete Signal Representation Standards (Pages 3–4, 2023-Q1.a)

MethodFormat & NotationClass Notes Formulation
1. Graphical Stem plot over integer index \(n\) Vertical stems at discrete coordinates \(n = -3, -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, 3, 4\).
2. Functional Piecewise conditional formula \(x(n) = \begin{cases} 1; & n = 1, 4, -1 \\ 2; & n = 2, -2 \\ -2; & n = -3 \\ -1; & n = 3 \\ 0; & \text{otherwise} \end{cases}\)
3. Sequential Amplitude sequence with origin arrow (\(\uparrow_0\)) \(x(n) = \{ -2, \, 2, \, 1, \, \underset{\uparrow_0}{0}, \, 1, \, 2, \, -1, \, 1 \}\)
Golden Rule: Arrow marks \(n=0\). If omitted, first element is at \(n=0\).
4. Tabular Discrete coordinate lookup table \[ \begin{array}{|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|} \hline n & -3 & -2 & -1 & 0 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 \\ \hline x[n] & -2 & 2 & 1 & 0 & 1 & 2 & -1 & 1 \\ \hline \end{array} \]

Elementary Discrete Sequences (Page 4, 2023-Q6.a & 2021-Q3.a)

• Unit Impulse: \(\delta(n) = 1\) at \(n=0\), \(0\) elsewhere. Left shift: \(\delta(n+1) = 1\) at \(n=-1\); Right shift: \(\delta(n-1) = 1\) at \(n=1\).
• Unit Step: \(u(n) = 1\) for \(n \ge 0\), \(0\) for \(n \lt 0\).
• Ramp: \(r(n) = n \cdot u(n) = n\) for \(n \ge 0\), \(0\) for \(n \lt 0\).
• Exponential: \(x(n) = a^n\); \(0 \lt |a| \lt 1 \implies \text{decaying exponential}\), \(|a| \gt 1 \implies \text{growing exponential}\).

Lecture 3 & 4 — Periodicity Tests & Energy/Power Analysis
3 Periodicity Problem Types & Infinite Series Energy/Power Determinations
Source: DSP Class Lecture Notes • Pages 5–10 (27/07/26 – 30/07/26)

3-Type Periodicity Test Shortcuts (Pages 6–7)

Problem TypeExample SignalStep-by-Step Test MethodVerdict
Type 1: Sum of \(\sin\) & \(\cos\) \(x(n) = \sin\frac{2\pi n}{3} + \cos\frac{2\pi n}{5}\) 1. Frequencies: \(2\pi f_1 = \frac{2\pi}{3} \implies f_1 = \frac{1}{3} \implies N_1 = 3\); \(2\pi f_2 = \frac{2\pi}{5} \implies f_2 = \frac{1}{5} \implies N_2 = 5\).
2. Ratio: \(\frac{N_1}{N_2} = \frac{3}{5}\) (Rational number).
3. Fundamental Period: \(N = \text{LCM}(N_1, N_2) = 15\). 		Periodic (\(N = 15\))
Type 2: Single \(\cos\) or \(\sin\) \(x(n) = \cos(4n)\) 1. Angular frequency: \(\omega = 4\).
2. Cyclic frequency: \(f = \frac{\omega}{2\pi} = \frac{4}{2\pi} = \frac{2}{\pi}\).
3. Since \(f\) is irrational (not an integer ratio), condition \(x(n) = x(n+N)\) can never be satisfied. 		Aperiodic
Type 3: Complex Exponential \(x(n) = e^{j \frac{7\pi n}{4}}\) 1. Periodicity condition: \(e^{j \frac{7\pi (n+N)}{4}} = e^{j \frac{7\pi n}{4}} \implies e^{j \frac{7\pi N}{4}} = 1\).
2. Set phase to integer multiples of \(2\pi\): \(\frac{7\pi N}{4} = 2\pi K \implies N = \frac{8K}{7}\).
3. Smallest integer \(K = 7 \implies N = 8\) samples. 		Periodic (\(N = 8\))

Energy vs Power Sequence Rules (Pages 8–10, 2024-Q5.b & 2021-Q1.d)

• Energy Sequence: \(E = \sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty} |x(n)|^2 \lt \infty\) and \(P = 0\).
• Power Sequence: \(P = \lim_{N\to\infty} \frac{1}{2N+1} \sum_{n=-N}^{N} |x(n)|^2 \lt \infty\) and \(E = \infty\).
• Infinite Sum Shortcut: \(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty} a^n = \frac{1}{1 - a}\) (for \(|a| \lt 1\)).

• For \(x[n] = (0.5)^n u[n]\): \(E = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} (0.25)^n = \frac{1}{1 - 0.25} = \frac{4}{3} \text{ J}\), \(P = 0 \implies \mathbf{Energy\ Signal}\).
• For \(x[n] = u[n]\): \(E = \infty\), \(P = \lim_{N\to\infty} \frac{N+1}{2N+1} = 0.5 \text{ W} \implies \mathbf{Power\ Signal}\).
• For \(x(t) = \cos(t)\) (2021-Q1.d): \(E = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \cos^2 t \, dt = \infty\), \(P = \lim_{T\to\infty} \frac{1}{2T} \int_{-T}^{T} \cos^2 t \, dt = 0.5 \implies \mathbf{Power\ Signal}\).
Lectures 5, 6 & 7 — System Synthesis & BIBO Stability
Signal Operations, SFG Block Synthesis & Absolute Summability Tests
Source: DSP Class Lecture Notes • Pages 11–19 (09/08/26 – 17/08/26)

1. Signal Operations Rules (Page 11, 2021-Q1.b)

• Folding: \(x(n) \longleftrightarrow x(-n)\).
• Addition (\(x_1 + x_2\)): If no origin arrow is marked, the 1st value is placed at origin \(n=0\).
• Time Shifting: Right shift \(x(n - k)\) delays sequence; Left shift \(x(n + k)\) advances sequence.

2. System Representations & Difference Equation Synthesis (Pages 12–13, 2020-Q2.a & 2023-Q1.b)

4 Basic Building Elements:
1. Adder: \(\bigoplus\)   2. Constant Multiplier: \(\xrightarrow{\quad K \quad}\)   3. Unit Delay: \(\boxed{z^{-1}}\)   4. Unit Advance: \(\boxed{z^k}\)
Synthesis of \(y(n) = x(n) + \frac{1}{4}y(n-1) + x(n-2)\):
Feedforward path processes present \(x(n)\) and delayed \(x(n-2)\) via two delays; Feedback recursive path taps \(y(n)\) through delay \(z^{-1}\), multiplies by \(\frac{1}{4}\), and feeds into master adder.

3. System Classifications & BIBO Stability Criterion (Pages 14–19, 2024-Q5.c)

ClassificationCriterion & Mathematical TestClass Notes Examples
Linear vs Non-Linear Superposition: Law of Additivity + Law of Homogeneity \(y(n) = x^2(n) \implies [x_1 + x_2]^2 \ne x_1^2 + x_2^2 \implies \mathbf{Non-Linear}\).
\(y(n) = x(n-1) + x(n) \implies \mathbf{Linear}\).
Time Invariant vs Variant Time shift in input yields identical shift in output without parameter alteration \(y(n) = x(n) \implies \mathbf{Time\ Invariant}\).
\(y(n) = n x(n) \implies \mathbf{Time\ Variant}\).
BIBO Stability Absolute Summability of Impulse Response: \(\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty} |h(n)| \lt \infty\) • \(h_1[n] = e^{n/2}u[n-4] \implies \sum_{n=4}^{\infty} e^{n/2} = \infty \implies \mathbf{Unstable}\).
• \(h_2[n] = 3^n u[-n] \implies \sum_{m=0}^{\infty} (1/3)^m = \frac{1}{1-1/3} = 1.5 \lt \infty \implies \mathbf{Stable}\).
• Continuous \(h(t) = e^{-2t}u(t) \implies \int_0^{\infty} e^{-2t} dt = 0.5 \lt \infty \implies \mathbf{Stable}\).
Lecture 8 — Sampling & Spectral Reconstruction
Nyquist Sampling Theorem, 3 Spectral Cases & Multi-Tone Decomposition
Source: DSP Class Lecture Notes • Pages 19–22 (31/08/26)

Nyquist Theorem Statement (Page 20, 2023-Q2.a):

"A continuous time signal can be completely reconstructed from its samples if the sampling frequency is at least twice the highest frequency component present in the signal:" \[f_s \ge 2 f_m \quad \implies \quad f_N = 2 f_m \text{ (Nyquist Rate)}\]

3 Critical Sampling Cases (Page 20, 2023-Q2.b)

CaseSampling ConditionSpectral Diagram BehaviourReconstruction Status
Case 1: Undersampling \(f_s \lt 2 f_m\) Adjacent spectral replicas overlap; high frequencies fold into baseband Reconstruction Impossible (Aliasing Distortion)
Case 2: Nyquist Rate \(f_s = 2 f_m\) Adjacent spectral replicas just touch at \(\pm f_m\) Theoretical Perfect Reconstruction (Requires Ideal Brick-Wall LPF)
Case 3: Oversampling \(f_s \gt 2 f_m\) Guard band exists between adjacent spectral replicas Practical Perfect Reconstruction (Achievable with Real Analog Filter)

Multi-Tone Analysis Shortcut (Page 21, 2021-Q(2d))

For composite multi-frequency signals \(x(t) = 3\cos(2000\pi t) + 4\sin(6000\pi t) + 8\cos(12000\pi t)\):
1. Extract component frequencies: \(f_1 = \frac{2000\pi}{2\pi} = 1000\text{ Hz}\), \(f_2 = \frac{6000\pi}{2\pi} = 3000\text{ Hz}\), \(f_3 = \frac{12000\pi}{2\pi} = 6000\text{ Hz}\).
2. Identify highest frequency: \(f_m = \max(f_1, f_2, f_3) = 6000\text{ Hz}\).
3. Minimum sampling frequency for perfect reconstruction: \(f_s \ge 2 f_m = 12,000\text{ Hz}\).
4. Discrete sequence at \(f_s = 5000\text{ samples/s}\): substitute \(t = n T_s = \frac{n}{5000}\) into \(x(t)\):
\[x[n] = 3 \cos\left(\frac{2\pi n}{5}\right) + 4 \sin\left(\frac{6\pi n}{5}\right) + 8 \cos\left(\frac{12\pi n}{5}\right)\]

Lecture 9 — Correlation Easy Methods
Multiplication Table Correlation & Output Origin Rules
Source: DSP Class Lecture Notes (Lectures 9–17) • Pages 1–5 (02/09/26)

Correlation Definition: Discrete-time signal-এর মধ্যে similarity নির্ণয়ের পদ্ধতি।

\[ r_{xx}[\ell] = \sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty} x[n] x[n-\ell] \quad (\text{Autocorrelation}) \] \[ r_{xy}[\ell] = \sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty} x[n] y[n-\ell] \quad (\text{Cross-correlation}) \]

Worked Example: Autocorrelation via Multiplication Table

Given: \(x[n] = [1, 2, 3, 4]\) with indices \(n = -1, 0, 1, 2\) (origin sample \(x[0] = 2\)).

Folded sequence: \(x[-n] = [4, 3, 2, 1]\) with indices \(n = -2, -1, 0, 1\) (origin sample \(x[-0] = 2\)).

\(x[n] \backslash x[-n]\)4321
14321
28642
312963
4161284

Anti-Diagonal Sum:

\[ r_{xx}[\ell] = [4,\ 8+3=11,\ 12+6+2=20,\ 16+9+4+1=\mathbf{30},\ 12+6+2=20,\ 8+3=11,\ 4] \] \[ r_{xx}[\ell] = [4, 11, 20, \mathbf{30}, 20, 11, 4] \]
Output Origin Shortcut Techniques:
  • Technique 1 (Zero-based Index Sum): \(x[n]\)-এর origin position \(1\) এবং \(x[-n]\)-এর origin position \(2\)। ফলে output origin index: \(1 + 2 = \mathbf{3}\) (অর্থাৎ ৪র্থ উপাদান \(30\) হলো \(\ell = 0\))।
  • Technique 2 (Starting Index Sum): \(x[n]\) শুরু হয় \(-1\) থেকে, \(x[-n]\) শুরু হয় \(-2\) থেকে। ফলে output শুরু হয়: \(-1 + (-2) = \mathbf{-3}\)।

Lecture 10 — Convolution Easy Methods
Matrix Method & 5-Step Graphical Convolution

1. Matrix Method (Multiplication Table)

Given: \(x[n] = [1, 2, 3, 4]\) (\(n = -2, -1, 0, 1\)), \(h[n] = [1, 4, 2, 1]\) (\(n = -1, 0, 1, 2\)).

\(x[n] \backslash h[n]\)1421
11421
22842
331263
441684
\[ y[n] = [1, 2+4=6, 3+8+2=13, 4+12+4+1=\mathbf{21}, 16+6+2=24, 8+3=11, 4] \] \[ y[n] = [1, 6, 13, \mathbf{21}, 24, 11, 4], \quad n = -3, -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, 3 \]

Starting index: \(-2 + (-1) = -3\). Origin sample: \(y[0] = 21\).

2. 5-Step Graphical Method

  1. Step 1 Dummy variable \(k\) ব্যবহার করো: \(x[k], h[k]\)।
  2. Step 2 Output index range নির্ণয় করো: \[ n_{\min} = -1 + 0 = -1, \quad L = M + N - 1 = 3 + 3 - 1 = 5, \quad n_{\max} = 1 + 2 = 3 \]
  3. Step 3 \(h[k]\) fold করে \(h[-k]\) তৈরি করো।
  4. Step 4 নির্দিষ্ট \(n\)-এর জন্য shift করে \(h[n-k]\) তৈরি করো।
  5. Step 5 Overlapping sample multiply করে যোগ করো:
Shift \(n\)Overlapping CalculationOutput \(y[n]\)
\(n = -1\)\(x[-1]h[0] = 3 \times 1\)3
\(n = 0\)\(x[-1]h[1] + x[0]h[0] = 3(3) + 1(1)\)10
\(n = 1\)\(x[-1]h[2] + x[0]h[1] + x[1]h[0] = 3(2) + 1(3) + 2(1)\)11
\(n = 2\)\(x[0]h[2] + x[1]h[1] = 1(2) + 2(3)\)8
\(n = 3\)\(x[1]h[2] = 2(2)\)4
Lecture 11 — Overlap-Add, Overlap-Save & Circular Convolution
Block Filtering (Overlap-Add vs. Overlap-Save) & Concentric Circles Method
Source: DSP Class Lecture Notes (Lectures 9–17) • Pages 9–14 (11/09/26)

1. Overlap-Add Method (Zero-Pad & Add Overlaps)

Given impulse response length \(L_h = 3\):

\[ N = 2L_h = 2(3) = 6 \quad (\text{Convolution Block Length}) \] \[ L = N - L_h + 1 = 6 - 3 + 1 = 4 \quad (\text{Input Subset Length}) \]

Input sequence-কে \(L=4\) দৈর্ঘ্যের ব্লকে ভাগ করে প্রতিটি ব্লকের শেষে \(L_h - 1 = 2\) টি শূন্য যোগ (zero-padding) করে দৈর্ঘ্য \(N = 6\) করতে হয়। প্রতিটি ব্লকের কনভোলিউশন রেজাল্টের ওভারল্যাপিং প্রান্তগুলো যোগ (Add) করা হয়।

Overlap-Add Summary Table:

BlockOffsetLinear Output (\(L=6\))Overlapped Samples Added
\(y_1[n]\)\(n = 0\)\([1, 4, 6, 6, \mathbf{1}, \mathbf{6}]\)Last 2 samples overlap with \(y_2\)
\(y_2[n]\)\(n = 4\)\([\mathbf{3}, \mathbf{4}, 2, -13, \mathbf{-11}, \mathbf{-3}]\)Overlap: \((1+3=4), (6+4=10)\)
\(y_3[n]\)\(n = 8\)\([\mathbf{1}, \mathbf{3}, 7, 6, 4, -3]\)Overlap: \((-11+1=-10), (-3+3=0)\)

2. Overlap-Save Method (Overlap Inputs & Discard Front Aliasing)

Core Rule for Overlap-Save:
ইনপুট ব্লক গঠনের সময় পূর্ববর্তী ব্লকের শেষ \(L_h - 1 = 2\) টি স্যাম্পল দিয়ে নতুন ব্লকের শুরু ওভারল্যাপ করা হয়। সার্কুলার কনভোলিউশনের পর প্রতি ব্লকের প্রথম \(L_h - 1 = 2\) টি অ্যালিয়াসড স্যাম্পল Discard (বর্জন) করা হয় এবং বাকি \(L = 4\) টি স্যাম্পল সরাসরি জোড়া (Concatenate) লাগানো হয়!

\[\text{Input Block: } x_i = [\underbrace{\text{Last } L_h-1 \text{ samples of } x_{i-1}}_{\text{Overlap (2 samples)}}, \underbrace{\text{New } L \text{ samples}}_{\text{Valid (4 samples)}}]\] \[\text{Output Block: } y_i = x_i \circledast_N h_N \implies \text{Discard first } L_h-1 \text{ samples, Keep remaining } L \text{ samples}\]
Method Input Segmentation Convolution Performed Output Reconstruction
Overlap-Add No overlap in input (non-overlapping segments of length \(L\)), zero-padded by \(L_h-1\) \(N\)-point linear / circular convolution Overlapping tail samples (\(L_h-1\)) are added together at block boundaries.
Overlap-Save Inputs overlap by \(L_h-1\) samples between consecutive blocks \(N\)-point circular convolution First \(L_h-1\) corrupted samples are discarded; remaining \(L\) samples are concatenated.

Exam Worked Example (2024-Q4.d): \(h[n]=[3,2,1]\), \(x[n]=[2,1,-1,-2,-3,5,6,-1,2,0,2,1]\), \(N=6\)

  • Blocks: \(x_1=[0,0,2,1,-1,-2]\), \(x_2=[-1,-2,-3,5,6,-1]\), \(x_3=[6,-1,2,0,2,1]\), \(x_4=[2,1,0,0,0,0]\)
  • Circular results \(\to\) Discard first 2 \(\to\) Kept parts: \([6,7,1,-7]\), \([-14,7,25,14]\), \([10,3,8,7]\), \([4,1]\)
  • Output: \(y[n] = \{6, 7, 1, -7, -14, 7, 25, 14, 10, 3, 8, 7, 4, 1\}\)
  • Sum Check: \(\sum x \cdot \sum h = 12 \times 6 = 72 = \sum y[n] \quad \checkmark\)

3. Circular Convolution via Concentric Circles & Modulo

\[ y_c[n] = x[n] \mathbin{Ⓝ} h[n] = \sum_{k=0}^{N-1} x[k] h[(n-k) \pmod N] \]

Concentric Circle Procedure:

  • Inner circle-এ \(x[k]\) sample-গুলো clockwise বসাও।
  • Outer circle-এ \(h[-k]\) counter-clockwise বসাও।
  • নির্দিষ্ট \(n\)-এর জন্য outer circle rotate করে প্রতি মুখোমুখি sample গুণ করে যোগ করো।
Lectures 12 & 13 — Standard Z-Transforms & Properties
Essential Standard Pairs & Differentiation Property Shortcut
Source: DSP Class Lecture Notes (Lectures 9–17) • Pages 15–21 (14/09/26 – 20/09/26)

Standard Sequence Transform Pairs:

Sequence \(x[n]\)Z-Transform \(X(z)\)Closed FormROC
\(\delta[n]\)\(1\)\(1\)Entire \(z\)-plane
\(u[n]\)\(\frac{1}{1-z^{-1}}\)\(\frac{z}{z-1}\)\(|z| \gt 1\)
\(a^n u[n]\)\(\frac{1}{1-a z^{-1}}\)\(\frac{z}{z-a}\)\(|z| \gt |a|\)
\(-a^n u[-n-1]\)\(\frac{1}{1-a z^{-1}}\)\(\frac{z}{z-a}\)\(|z| \lt |a|\)
\(n a^n u[n]\)\(\frac{a z^{-1}}{(1-a z^{-1})^2}\)\(\frac{a z}{(z-a)^2}\)\(|z| \gt |a|\)
\(\cos(\omega_0 n) u[n]\)\(\frac{1 - z^{-1}\cos\omega_0}{1 - 2z^{-1}\cos\omega_0 + z^{-2}}\)\(\frac{z(z-\cos\omega_0)}{z^2 - 2z\cos\omega_0 + 1}\)\(|z| \gt 1\)
\(\sin(\omega_0 n) u[n]\)\(\frac{z^{-1}\sin\omega_0}{1 - 2z^{-1}\cos\omega_0 + z^{-2}}\)\(\frac{z\sin\omega_0}{z^2 - 2z\cos\omega_0 + 1}\)\(|z| \gt 1\)

Differentiation Property Shortcut:

\[ \mathcal{Z}\{n x[n]\} = -z \frac{dX(z)}{dz} = z^{-1} \frac{dX(z)}{dz^{-1}} \]
Lecture 15 — Inverse Z-Transform & ROC Rules
The \(X(z)/z\) Partial Fraction Shortcut & ROC Selection Rules
Source: DSP Class Lecture Notes (Lectures 9–17) • Pages 22–29 (28/09/26)

The \(X(z)/z\) Partial Fraction Method:

Standard partial fractions \(X(z) = \frac{A}{1-p z^{-1}}\) করলে residue বের করা জটিল হয়। সহজ শর্টকাট মেথড:

\[ \frac{X(z)}{z} = \frac{A}{z - p_1} + \frac{B}{z - p_2} \] \[ \implies X(z) = A \frac{z}{z - p_1} + B \frac{z}{z - p_2} \]

উভয় পাশে \(z\) গুণ করলে সরাসরি standard pair \(\frac{z}{z-p} \leftrightarrow p^n u[n]\) চলে আসে!

ROC Selection Rules:
  • Causal Signal (\(n \ge 0\)): ROC is exterior to outermost pole, \(|z| \gt |p_{\max}|\). Output contains \(u[n]\).
  • Anti-Causal Signal (\(n \lt 0\)): ROC is interior to innermost pole, \(|z| \lt |p_{\min}|\). Output contains \(-u[-n-1]\).
  • Two-Sided Signal: ROC is an annular ring \(|p_1| \lt |z| \lt |p_2|\).
  • Finite Duration Sequence:
    • Causal (\(n = 0 \dots N-1\)): ROC is \(0 \lt |z| \le \infty\) (entire plane except \(z = 0\)).
    • Anti-causal (\(n = -(N-1) \dots 0\)): ROC is \(0 \le |z| \lt \infty\) (entire plane except \(z = \infty\)).
    • Two-sided: ROC is \(0 \lt |z| \lt \infty\) (entire plane except \(z = 0\) and \(z = \infty\)).
Lecture 17 — DFT & Twiddle Factor Matrix
4-Point DFT Matrix (\(F_4\)) & IDFT Shortcut Method
Source: DSP Class Lecture Notes (Lectures 9–17) • Pages 30–36 (04/10/26)

1. Twiddle Factor (\(W_N\)) Definition & Periodic Values:

\[ W_N = e^{-j\frac{2\pi}{N}} \] \[ W_4^0 = 1, \quad W_4^1 = -j, \quad W_4^2 = -1, \quad W_4^3 = j \]

2. The 4-Point DFT Matrix Equation:

\[ \mathbf{X} = F_4 \mathbf{x} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -j & -1 & j \\ 1 & -1 & 1 & -1 \\ 1 & j & -1 & -j \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x[0] \\ x[1] \\ x[2] \\ x[3] \end{bmatrix} \]

3. The 4-Point IDFT Matrix Equation:

\[ \mathbf{x} = \frac{1}{4} F_4^H \mathbf{X} = \frac{1}{4} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & j & -1 & -j \\ 1 & -1 & 1 & -1 \\ 1 & -j & -1 & j \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} X[0] \\ X[1] \\ X[2] \\ X[3] \end{bmatrix} \]

Exam Advantage: DFT বা IDFT calculation formula দিয়ে sum আকারে না লিখে সরাসরি \(F_4\) বা \(F_4^H\) matrix গুণ করলে 1 মিনিটে 100% নির্ভুল সমাধান পাওয়া যায়!

Lecture 18 — Radix-2 FFT Butterflies & Duality
4-Point & 8-Point DIT/DIF FFT Butterflies, Bit-Reversal Tables & Duality Rules
Source: DSP Class Lecture Notes • Pages 50–52 (12/10/26)

1. Bit-Reversal Permutation Standards (Page 50)

In Radix-2 DIT FFT algorithms, input data must be arranged in bit-reversed order:

Normal Index \(n\)Binary \((b_1 b_0)\)Bit-Reversed \((b_0 b_1)\)Reversed Index \(n_{br}\)8-Point Index Mapping (\(N=8\))
000000\(0 \to 0, \quad 1 \to 4\)
101102\(2 \to 2, \quad 3 \to 6\)
210011\(4 \to 1, \quad 5 \to 5\)
311113\(6 \to 3, \quad 7 \to 7\)

2. 4-Point DIT FFT Butterfly Flow Graph (Page 50)

Inputs (Bit-Reversed): \(x[0], x[2], x[1], x[3]\)   Outputs (Normal): \(X[0], X[1], X[2], X[3]\)
• Stage 1 Twiddle: \(W_4^0 = 1\)
• Stage 2 Twiddles: \(W_4^0 = 1, \quad W_4^1 = -j\)
• Butterfly Equations: \(X = A + B W_N^r, \quad Y = A - B W_N^r\).
For \(x[n] = \{1, 0, 0, 1\}\): Stage 1 yields \(\{1, 1, 1, -j\}\); Stage 2 yields \(X[k] = \{2, 1+j, 0, 1-j\}\).

3. DIT IFFT Structure (Page 51)

Inputs in normal order \(\{X[0], X[1], X[2], X[3]\}\); inverse twiddle factor \(W_4^{-1} = j\); outputs scaled by \(\frac{1}{N} = \frac{1}{4}\) emerge in bit-reversed order: \(x[0]=1, x[2]=0, x[1]=0, x[3]=1 \implies x[n] = \{1, 0, 0, 1\}\).

4. FFT/IFFT Duality Relations (Page 52)

\[\mathbf{DIT\ FFT} \sim \mathbf{DIF\ IFFT} \qquad \text{and} \qquad \mathbf{DIF\ FFT} \sim \mathbf{DIT\ IFFT}\]

Exam Shortcut Rule: If an exam problem asks to calculate IDFT or IFFT, you can directly use the forward FFT butterfly architecture with conjugated twiddle factors (\(W_N^*\) or \(W_N^{-r}\)) and multiply final outputs by \(\frac{1}{N}\)!
